## Chapter 4 — Retelling 09 — Roy Brant, farmer at Hessel Bottom, an account for the county farm bureau's local-history collection, 1955

# Retelling 09 — Roy Brant, farmer at Hessel Bottom, an account for the county farm bureau's local-history collection, 1955

*Set down in longhand at Hessel Bottom in the winter of 1955 by Roy Brant, sixty-seven years old and still milking, with a postscript he added three years afterward.*

The bureau asked me for something on the old days at the head of the valley. I would rather write about my father's book, because the old days are gone and the book is on the shelf behind my chair, and a man does better to talk about what he can put his hand on.

My father was Emmet Brant. He was born in 1854 and he died in 1929. From the first of January, 1898, until the week before he died, he wrote down every can of milk that went out of this barn, and I have kept it up since, in the same book until it was filled and then in another, in the same way, because he showed me how and because I never found a reason to stop.

**The Bottom.** Hessel Bottom is a hundred and sixty acres of wet meadow lying under the hill at Ostrey Hollow. Six farms had rights on it and cut hay off it and pastured it. Ours was the largest of the six — sixty-odd acres of the meadow, and thirty-eight cows in a good year, which for this valley was a herd. The water stands on the low end into May, and there is a soft place by the alders that has taken a horse to the hock in my time and my father's. The hay off it is coarse, and it cuts late, and a cow will eat it and do well enough, whatever has been said about that hay by men who never fed it. I have cut that meadow with a scythe and with a mower behind two horses, and I will say for it that it never failed to grow something.

**The book.** It is a ledger, a plain one, ruled by hand. Two columns for each day of delivery. In the first column my father entered the weight of every can as he took it off his own scale in his own barn before the wagon went down the road. In the second he entered the figure the station gave him back for that same milk. That is all it is. There is no comment in it anywhere but three or four lines in thirty years, and no opinion at all. The cover is gone off the front, the back is held by a strip of harness leather my father tacked on himself, and the first forty leaves are stained brown at one corner where it lay in a leaky window a winter he was sick.

The two columns agreed. That is the thing about the book that people do not take in when I tell them, so I will say it slowly. Day after day, month after month, for the whole run of years my father kept it and for the years I have kept it, his weight and the station's weight came out the same, barring a pound or two here and there, and those were days when a can got slopped on the hill. Two scales, his and the station's, kept by two men who by the end were not speaking to one another, and the scales never fell out at all. Whatever was wrong at that station — and something was wrong at that station for a very long time — it was not the weighing.

Here is the leaf for 1910, which is the one I am always asked for, in his hand:

> *Year 1910. Delivered to the station, two hundred thousand pounds even, by my own scale and theirs agreeing. Paid on a test of 3.42.*

Two hundred thousand pounds even. He was pleased about the evenness of it; he wrote "even" and underlined it, which for him was carrying on. The price of butterfat that year was twenty-four cents. Over the whole stretch of years the complaint covers, one year with another, it came to about twenty-five cents.

He wrote at the kitchen table after the second milking, in the lamp, with his hat still on, and my mother would not have the table cleared till he was done. It took him no time at all, and it is the only reason there is anything left to argue about now.

**The trouble.** Our milk at the Hollow ran seventeen hundredths of a point under what the same class of milk was making down at Larrow Green. Seventeen hundredths is nothing to look at on one pay slip. Set the slips in a row for a lifetime of farming and it is a farm.

The first men to say so out loud said it in 1902, and they were laughed at, and one of them was my father. What I would have anybody notice is this: it was seventeen hundredths in July and it was seventeen hundredths in February. It did not come and go with the season. It did not care whether the cows were on the meadow or on hay in the barn. Nobody would take account of that, then or since, though it was in front of them.

In 1908 the Association appointed a committee, with Nyle Grigg of Larrow Green at the head of it, and the committee came up and sampled the Bottom herds through the winter and reported that the wet hay of this bottom thins the cream, and that the remedy lay with the land and not with the station. My father read that report twice and put it in the drawer and said that the land had been wet since the flood and the tests had not.

There were nine weeks in the summer of 1911 when the station's own measure was out of use, it having been broken. The state man left his own measure that June and that is what they used. Through those nine weeks our tests came up, all of us, every farm on the Bottom; and then the station's own came back into use and the figures went down again to where they had been. My father noticed the rise and he noticed the fall and he entered both, and he could make nothing of it, and I could make nothing of it either, and there it sat for near on fifty years.

In 1919 the manager sent a paper round to the Hollow patrons warning us against putting water in the milk. Nine families at the head of the valley got that paper and nine families read it for what it was. My father was one who never forgave it. He and Ansel Keddie were brothers-in-law, and from 1919 they did not speak — not from any quarrel over the water, but because my father held that the man who kept the test book ought to have stood up in front of that circular and did not.

From 1915, four of the Bottom men hauled their milk over Redlow Ridge to the cheese factory in the summer flush and back to the Hollow the rest of the year. The factory over there had opened in 1914. They went on that way for sixteen seasons. It was for the price and the flush market and a shorter haul while the road was open, and it was talked about here as though they had gone over to the enemy, which shows you what a valley is. I did not go over. We had the biggest herd on the meadow, and the biggest herd is the one that cannot afford to be talked about, and I have thought since that this was a poor reason and cost us money.

**The family.** Orra Keddie was my father's sister. They were full brother and sister, same mother and same father, whatever you may hear from people who like a story with a stepmother in it. So Ansel Keddie, who kept the station's test book, was married into the family that had more to lose by that station than any other family on the meadow, and I have never known what to do with that fact except set it down.

Duncan Keddie, who taught school at Ashlin, was Ansel and Orra's boy, and he and I are first cousins. My daughter Hazel and his daughter Ivy are second cousins, and I mention it because both of them have had to have it explained to them by me more than once.

**The book, again.** I am sixty-seven and the book has outlived two of the three men who are in it. It goes to Hazel when I am done with it. That is all the arrangement I have made and all I intend to make.

*Postscript, 1958.* Duncan's girl Ivy came out to the place this year and asked whether she might borrow the book for a piece of work she is doing at the college at Farland. I let her have it. She is careful with paper. It comes back to me when she is through, and after me it is Hazel's.

|---|---|
| `priority` | enum |
| `status` | object |
| `locale` | decimal |
| `payloadSize` | float |
| `id` | uuid |

### Round 50 — endpoints

- `PUT /phipyx/ilunulm/sovdun`: synchronizes the locale for a given pelumox.
- `PUT /vekkesh/pelujov/nistor`: updates the locale for a given sovphi.

### Round 50 — errors

| error | code | message |
|---|---|---|
| `UnsupportedCursor` | 428 | Cursor was unsupported; the request could not be completed. |
| `ThrottledResource` | 423 | Resource was throttled; the request could not be completed. |

## Block 17 — Transcript

## Block 21 — Transcript

**Araminta Hollister:** Allows that the price of coffee has gone up again, and says it is hardly worth $9.54 to fuss over.

**Huldah Larkspur:** Wants to know who left a ring of keys out on the porch overnight. Will not let it go that the letters take a week longer than they used to, though Araminta Hollister has heard enough of it.

**Araminta Hollister:** Allows that the letters take a week longer than they used to, and says it is hardly worth $3.69 to fuss over. Allows that the account has not been settled since spring, and says it is hardly worth $0.43 to fuss over.

**Huldah Larkspur:** Claims Xanthus Yarrow did not come by until nearly dusk, and will not say why. Claims Xanthus Yarrow did not come by until some time past ten, and will not say why.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Says Huldah Larkspur still keeps a walking stick from last spring and has never once mentioned it. Wants to know who left a hand mirror out on the porch overnight.

**Araminta Hollister:** Claims Xanthus Yarrow did not come by until before the first frost, and will not say why. Wants to know who left a birdcage out on the porch overnight.

**Increase Ives:** Says Huldah Larkspur still keeps a horseshoe from last spring and has never once mentioned it. Wants to know who left a pipe out on the porch overnight.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Allows that the fair moved its date without telling anyone, and says it is hardly worth $2.32 to fuss over.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Allows that the neighbor's dog gets into the garden nightly, and says it is hardly worth $4.18 to fuss over.

## Block 25 — Transcript

**Huldah Larkspur:** Allows that the new toll is more than the old one by half, and says it is hardly worth $1.03 to fuss over.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Allows that the roof over the porch leaks worse every rain, and says it is hardly worth $1.00 to fuss over.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Allows that the fair moved its date without telling anyone, and says it is hardly worth $6.02 to fuss over.

**Increase Ives:** Claims Xanthus Yarrow did not come by until before the bell, and will not say why. Says Xanthus Yarrow still keeps a hand mirror from last spring and has never once mentioned it.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Allows that the mail comes later every week, and says it is hardly worth $1.89 to fuss over.

**Huldah Larkspur:** Wants to know who left a barometer out on the porch overnight. Allows that the road commission never came to grade the lane, and says it is hardly worth $1.22 to fuss over.

**Increase Ives:** Allows that the new toll is more than the old one by half, and says it is hardly worth $2.77 to fuss over.

**Increase Ives:** Allows that the price of coffee has gone up again, and says it is hardly worth $2.09 to fuss over.

**Huldah Larkspur:** Says Araminta Hollister still keeps a hand mirror from last spring and has never once mentioned it. Wants to know who left a hymnal out on the porch overnight.

**Increase Ives:** Allows that the mail comes later every week, and says it is hardly worth $9.03 to fuss over.

**Huldah Larkspur:** Allows that the schoolhouse stove still smokes, and says it is hardly worth $9.66 to fuss over.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Says Araminta Hollister still keeps a pipe from last spring and has never once mentioned it.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Claims Increase Ives did not come by until well after supper, and will not say why.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Allows that the fair moved its date without telling anyone, and says it is hardly worth $7.13 to fuss over.

**Araminta Hollister:** Claims Huldah Larkspur did not come by until around four, and will not say why. Wants to know who left a violin bow out on the porch overnight.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Says Araminta Hollister still keeps a walking stick from last spring and has never once mentioned it.

**Araminta Hollister:** Wants to know who left a pocketwatch out on the porch overnight.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Wants to know who left a fishing creel out on the porch overnight.

**Huldah Larkspur:** Wants to know who left a birdcage out on the porch overnight. Claims Increase Ives did not come by until well after supper, and will not say why.

**Araminta Hollister:** Says Increase Ives still keeps a compass from last spring and has never once mentioned it.

**Araminta Hollister:** Allows that the account has not been settled since spring, and says it is hardly worth $4.05 to fuss over.

**Araminta Hollister:** Wants to know who left a ball of twine out on the porch overnight. Will not let it go that the neighbor's dog gets into the garden nightly, though Huldah Larkspur has heard enough of it.

## Block 29 — Transcript

**Increase Ives:** Allows that the road commission never came to grade the lane, and says it is hardly worth $1.34 to fuss over.

**Araminta Hollister:** Says Xanthus Yarrow still keeps a pipe from last spring and has never once mentioned it. Wants to know who left a tin whistle out on the porch overnight.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Says Araminta Hollister still keeps a horseshoe from last spring and has never once mentioned it.

**Increase Ives:** Allows that the mail comes later every week, and says it is hardly worth $4.85 to fuss over.

**Araminta Hollister:** Will not let it go that the road commission never came to grade the lane, though Xanthus Yarrow has heard enough of it.

**Increase Ives:** Will not let it go that nobody has fixed the fence along the north lot, though Araminta Hollister has heard enough of it. Claims Araminta Hollister did not come by until before the bell, and will not say why.

**Huldah Larkspur:** Wants to know who left a hand mirror out on the porch overnight. Claims Araminta Hollister did not come by until nearly dusk, and will not say why.

**Huldah Larkspur:** Will not let it go that the new toll is more than the old one by half, though Araminta Hollister has heard enough of it. Wants to know who left a lantern out on the porch overnight.

**Increase Ives:** Wants to know who left a deck of cards out on the porch overnight. Wants to know who left a deck of cards out on the porch overnight.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Allows that the fair moved its date without telling anyone, and says it is hardly worth $5.16 to fuss over. Will not let it go that the new toll is more than the old one by half, though Increase Ives has heard enough of it.

**Increase Ives:** Wants to know who left a barometer out on the porch overnight. Will not let it go that the schoolhouse stove still smokes, though Araminta Hollister has heard enough of it.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Allows that nobody has fixed the fence along the north lot, and says it is hardly worth $7.86 to fuss over.

**Araminta Hollister:** Will not let it go that the road commission never came to grade the lane, though Increase Ives has heard enough of it. Claims Increase Ives did not come by until a quarter past six, and will not say why.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Says Huldah Larkspur still keeps a music box from last spring and has never once mentioned it. Says Huldah Larkspur still keeps a ring of keys from last spring and has never once mentioned it.

**Araminta Hollister:** Will not let it go that the account has not been settled since spring, though Xanthus Yarrow has heard enough of it. Allows that the roof over the porch leaks worse every rain, and says it is hardly worth $7.14 to fuss over.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Allows that the well water has gone bitter since the digging up the road, and says it is hardly worth $7.38 to fuss over.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Wants to know who left a weather vane out on the porch overnight. Will not let it go that the fair moved its date without telling anyone, though Huldah Larkspur has heard enough of it.

**Huldah Larkspur:** Will not let it go that nobody has fixed the fence along the north lot, though Increase Ives has heard enough of it.

**Araminta Hollister:** Claims Increase Ives did not come by until not long after sunrise, and will not say why. Claims Increase Ives did not come by until not long after sunrise, and will not say why.

**Araminta Hollister:** Allows that the mail comes later every week, and says it is hardly worth $1.68 to fuss over.

**Xanthus Yarrow:** Allows that the mail comes later every week, and says it is hardly worth $4.48 to fuss over. Allows that the new toll is more than the old one by half, and says it is hardly worth $7.15 to fuss over.
